## Appendix 0.A Discussion

### 0.A.1 Open Source Code

To ensure the correctness, transparency, and reproducibility of our
findings, we will release the complete source code of our framework upon
publication. We hope this open-source release will enable the community
to independently verify our results, reproduce our experiments, and
build upon our methodology in a reliable and rigorous manner. More
importantly, we view this effort not merely as a matter of research
transparency, but as a way to foster broader engagement with what we
believe is a promising new research direction. By making our
implementation publicly accessible, we aim to lower the barrier for
follow-up work, facilitate fair comparison, encourage extension to new
settings, and support future contributions from researchers interested
in advancing this line of inquiry.

### 0.A.2 What Distinguishes ESC from Existing Self-Correction Methods

This is that it does not treat self-correction as a capability that must
be newly taught to Vision Language Models (VLMs) through costly
post-training \[[17](#bib.bib40), [18](#bib.bib37)\], dense supervision
\[[89](#bib.bib36)\], or carefully engineered high-quality feedback
\[[12](#bib.bib28), [99](#bib.bib27)\]. Much of the prior literature
improves self-correction by optimizing reflective behaviors via
Reinforcement Learning  \[[68](#bib.bib32), [20](#bib.bib33),
[86](#bib.bib34)\], supervised fine-tuning  \[[34](#bib.bib14),
[39](#bib.bib29)\], preference learning  \[[19](#bib.bib30),
[30](#bib.bib31)\], or curated correction trajectories
 \[[69](#bib.bib35), [102](#bib.bib39), [81](#bib.bib38)\], which makes
the resulting gains both resource-intensive and often sensitive to
feedback quality \[[47](#bib.bib41), [96](#bib.bib42)\]. In contrast,
ESC starts from a different premise: the self-correction ability is
already latent within VLMs, but remains under-activated in standard
inference and is further constrained by the intrinsic self-correction
blind spot \[[85](#bib.bib45), [33](#bib.bib46)\]. Rather than training
models to acquire a new corrective skill, ESC seeks to activate this
dormant capability at inference time. Concretely, inspired by the human
observation that emotional cues can prompt more cautious reflection, ESC
uses an external verifier to determine when revision is necessary and
then injects emotion-aware feedback that encourages the target VLM to
slow down, reassess its prior reasoning, and produce a more reliable
answer. From this perspective, ESC repositions self-correction from a
problem of teaching models how to revise to a problem of eliciting and
regulating the corrective potential they already possess, yielding a
lightweight, plug-and-play, and training-free alternative to existing
approaches. More broadly, we believe ESC represents a pioneering step
toward revisiting how VLMs can reliably self-correct effectively at
inference time: not by teaching self-correction as an external skill,
but by uncovering and activating the intrinsic self-correction ability
already embedded within the model.

### 0.A.3 How Emotional Self-Correction (![[Uncaptioned image]](2607.02089v1/figure/icon.png)ESC ) Actually Works: A Visualization

TL;DR: ![[Uncaptioned image]](2607.02089v1/figure/icon.png)ESC is
fundamentally a training-free self-correction framework in which the
target VLM revises its own response under feedback, rather than
acquiring self-correction through expensive post-training. In contrast
to prior methods that depend on resource-intensive optimization or
carefully curated high-quality feedback to turn on self-correction
ability, ESC starts from the hypothesis that such ability is already
latent within VLMs. We find that emotional feedback provides a simple
yet powerful mechanism for activating this internal capacity,
encouraging the model to re-evaluate its initial judgment in a more
stable and deliberate manner. Consequently, ESC reframes self-correction
not as a skill that must be externally taught, but as an intrinsic
capability that can be effectively elicited at inference time.

![Refer to caption](2607.02089v1/figure/supp_fig1.png)

Figure 9: Example of the ESC self-correction workflow. The target VLM
first produces an initial answer to the image-question pair. Rather than
directly replacing this answer, ESC verifies whether self-correction is
needed. If the initial response is judged unreliable, the verifier
provides emotional feedback to trigger a second-pass revision, allowing
the same model to reconsider its own prediction and generate a revised
response. The final output is then selected based on the verifier’s
comparison between the initial and revised responses.

The mechanism of Emotional Self-Correction (ESC) can be understood most
clearly through the car-stripe example shown in Fig.
 [9](#Pt0.A1.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 0.A.3 How Emotional Self-Correction (ESC ) Actually Works: A Visualization ‣ Appendix 0.A Discussion ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
Given an input image (I) and a question (Q), namely "Is the stripe in
the middle of the car blue or white?”, the target vision-language model
$`M_{T}`$ first responds in its standard manner and produces an initial
answer $`R_{initial}`$. In this case, the initial prediction is “It’s
blue.”. The verifier model $`M_{V}`$ then evaluates whether this initial
answer is acceptable. Here, the verifier $`M_{V}`$ determines that the
response is likely incorrect because the stripe in the image is actually
white rather than blue. At this point, instead of directly providing the
correct answer or explicit reasoning steps, ESC translates the
verifier’s negative judgment into an emotional feedback signal
$`F_{{emotional}}`$. In the example, this feedback takes the form of a
statement such as “I’m feeling really sad and disappointed right now.”
This design choice is central to ESC: the method does not correct the
target model by supplying the solution, but by introducing an affective
cue that signals that something has gone wrong and that the answer
should be reconsidered more carefully.

After receiving this emotional feedback $`F_{{emotional}}`$, the target
model $`M_{T}`$ is prompted again with the original image-question pair
together with the injected emotional context. Under this new condition,
the model revisits the same visual evidence and generates a revised
response $`R_{revised}`$. In the running example, the model changes its
answer from “blue” to “white.” This second response reflects the
self-correction stage of ESC, where the emotional signal functions as a
lightweight trigger for deeper re-examination rather than as an explicit
correction template. Finally, the verifier compares the initial and
revised answers and selects the one that is more appropriate as the
final output $`R_{\text{decided}}`$. In this example, the revised answer
is chosen because it is consistent with the image content. Viewed step
by step, the example shows that ESC operates as a selective refinement
loop: the target model first answers normally, the verifier checks
whether revision is needed, emotional feedback is generated only when
the initial answer is judged unsatisfactory, and the model is then given
a second opportunity to correct itself. This makes ESC different from
standard self-correction pipelines that rely mainly on direct textual
critique or explicit chain-of-thought revision, because ESC uses
emotion-like feedback as the intervention signal that encourages the
model to reassess its own prediction without being told the correct
answer in advance.

### 0.A.4 Promising Future Research Directions

Beyond the immediate gains of ESC, we believe this work opens a broader
research direction at the intersection of self-correction and
emotion-aware control for VLMs. Our results suggest that emotional cues
are not merely affective artifacts, but structured signals that can
regulate model behavior, trigger more cautious reasoning, and activate
latent self-correction abilities already embedded in VLMs. This points
to several promising avenues for future work.

- •
  First, self-correction could be extended from a single revision step
  to multi-turn, conversational self-correction, where models revise
  their responses more naturally over the course of an interaction,
  closer to how humans reflect and adjust under different emotional and
  social contexts.
- •
  Second, rather than using a fixed emotional strategy, future systems
  may benefit from context-adaptive emotion selection, choosing the most
  effective affective signal based on the question type, uncertainty
  level, task domain, or failure mode.
- •
  Third, our findings suggest that emotion should no longer be viewed
  solely as a capability to recognize or express, but also as a missing
  component in the broader inference pipeline of intelligent systems—one
  that may help mediate when, why, and how a model should reconsider its
  own reasoning.

More broadly, we see a promising opportunity to study the deeper
interaction between machine reasoning and emotion-like control
mechanisms, with the goal of developing VLMs that are not only more
capable, but also more reliable, controllable, and self-corrective in
realistic settings.

## Appendix 0.B Theoretical Foundations for Emotion Modeling

A central design decision in ESC is the choice of how to model and
operationalize emotions as self-correction signals. This section
provides a detailed justification for adopting Russell’s Circumplex
Model of Affect \[[73](#bib.bib24)\] and for partitioning the affective
space into exactly four quadrants. We first review the major paradigms
for representing emotions (Sec.
 [0.B.1](#Pt0.A2.SS1 "0.B.1 Major Paradigms for Modeling Emotions ‣ Appendix 0.B Theoretical Foundations for Emotion Modeling ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
then explain why a dimensional approach is best suited to our setting
(Sec.
 [0.B.2](#Pt0.A2.SS2 "0.B.2 Why Using A Dimensional Model: Selection of Russell’s Circumplex Theory ‣ Appendix 0.B Theoretical Foundations for Emotion Modeling ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
and finally justify the four-quadrant design (Sec.
 [0.B.3](#Pt0.A2.SS3 "0.B.3 Why Four Quadrants ‣ Appendix 0.B Theoretical Foundations for Emotion Modeling ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
).

### 0.B.1 Major Paradigms for Modeling Emotions

Discrete (Categorical) Models:

The most widely recognized categorical framework is Ekman’s Basic
Emotion Theory \[[22](#bib.bib83)\], which posits six universal
emotions—happiness, sadness, fear, anger, surprise, and disgust—each
associated with a distinct facial expression, physiological signature,
and evolutionary function. Plutchik \[[66](#bib.bib84)\] extends this
idea to eight primary emotions (joy, trust, fear, surprise, sadness,
disgust, anger, anticipation) arranged in a wheel-like structure with
opposing pairs and combinatorial “dyads.” These models are intuitive and
have been highly influential in affective computing and facial
expression recognition. However, they suffer from several limitations
when applied to our setting. First, the number and identity of “basic”
emotions remain contested: Ekman proposes six, Plutchik eight, and
subsequent work \[[15](#bib.bib93)\] has argued for as many as 27
experiential categories. This lack of consensus makes it difficult to
design a systematic and reproducible experimental protocol. Second,
discrete categories impose hard boundaries between emotional states, yet
empirical evidence consistently shows that emotions blend and
overlap \[[73](#bib.bib24), [4](#bib.bib87)\]. Third, for our purpose of
injecting emotional context into VLM prompts, a categorical approach
provides no principled mechanism for _systematic coverage_: one can only
enumerate a fixed list without guaranteeing that the selected emotions
span the full affective range.

Dimensional Models:

An alternative paradigm represents emotions as coordinates in a
continuous low-dimensional space. Russell’s Circumplex Model of
Affect \[[73](#bib.bib24)\] organizes affective states along two
orthogonal bipolar dimensions: _valence_ (pleasure–displeasure) and
_arousal_ (activation–deactivation). Barrett and
Russell \[[4](#bib.bib87)\] later confirmed through a comprehensive
review that two dimensions—pleasure and activation—reliably emerge
across self-reported feelings, cross-cultural affect semantics, and
facial expression ratings, establishing a broad consensus on the
two-dimensional structure of core affect. Watson and
Tellegen \[[88](#bib.bib85)\] proposed a related two-factor model with
axes labeled Positive Affect and Negative Affect, which can be shown to
be a 45° rotation of Russell’s valence–arousal axes \[[4](#bib.bib87),
[95](#bib.bib95)\]. Mehrabian and Russell \[[56](#bib.bib86)\] extended
the dimensional approach to three dimensions
(Pleasure–Arousal–Dominance, or PAD), but the third dimension
(dominance) has consistently accounted for substantially less variance
than the first two \[[73](#bib.bib24), [4](#bib.bib87)\], and its
inclusion complicates experimental design without proportionate benefit.

Appraisal-Based Models:

Scherer’s Component Process Model (CPM) \[[75](#bib.bib88),
[77](#bib.bib89)\] conceptualizes emotions as emergent, dynamic
processes arising from multi-level cognitive appraisals of stimulus
events. The CPM decomposes the appraisal process into sequential checks
(relevance, implications, coping potential, normative significance),
each involving multiple evaluation dimensions. While theoretically rich,
appraisal-based models require modeling the internal cognitive process
that gives rise to an emotion, which is not directly observable or
controllable in our setting. Since ESC operates by injecting emotional
_expressions_ (surface-level affective cues) into prompts rather than
manipulating the model’s internal appraisal mechanisms, the full
complexity of appraisal-based models is neither necessary nor directly
applicable.

Constructionist Models:

Barrett’s Theory of Constructed Emotion \[[6](#bib.bib90)\] argues that
discrete emotion categories are not biologically hardwired but are
instead actively constructed by the brain through the combination of
core affect (valence and arousal) with conceptual knowledge and
contextual cues. Importantly, even constructionist accounts retain the
valence–arousal space as the foundational substrate of affective
experience \[[5](#bib.bib91), [4](#bib.bib87)\]. This convergence across
otherwise competing theoretical perspectives strengthens the case for a
dimensional representation grounded in valence and arousal.

### 0.B.2 Why Using A Dimensional Model: Selection of Russell’s Circumplex Theory

Given the landscape described above, we adopt Russell’s Circumplex Model
of Affect \[[73](#bib.bib24)\] for three reasons:

Cross-paradigm consensus on two dimensions:

Despite deep theoretical disagreements between discrete, dimensional,
appraisal-based, and constructionist accounts, all converge on the
conclusion that valence and arousal are the two most fundamental
dimensions of affective experience \[[4](#bib.bib87),
[67](#bib.bib25)\]. The two-dimensional structure has been replicated
across cultures \[[71](#bib.bib92)\], languages, and measurement
modalities (self-report, facial expression, physiological signals). This
makes it the most robust and defensible foundation for an experimental
protocol that must generalize across diverse VLM architectures.

Systematic and reproducible coverage:

A continuous two-dimensional space provides a principled mechanism for
exhaustive and balanced coverage of the affective landscape. Rather than
arbitrarily selecting from a contested inventory of discrete emotions,
we can partition the space geometrically and sample representative
emotional expressions from each region. This ensures that our
experimental design is not biased toward any particular subset of the
emotional spectrum and enables controlled comparison across affective
conditions.

Operational suitability for emotional feedback construction:

Each location in the valence–arousal space corresponds to a recognizable
emotional tone (e.g., high-valence/high-arousal $`\to`$ excitement or
enthusiasm; low-valence/low-arousal $`\to`$ sadness or melancholy). This
direct correspondence allows ESC to translate affective coordinates into
natural-language emotional cues that serve as _emotional feedback_
signals during the self-correction stage. Because ESC communicates
affective context through textual interaction with the target VLM, the
circumplex representation provides a simple and interpretable mechanism
for conditioning the model’s reasoning process through emotionally
grounded feedback.

Why not the PAD (3D) model?

The Pleasure–Arousal–Dominance model \[[56](#bib.bib86)\] adds a third
dimension (dominance/control), which could in principle capture whether
an emotional cue conveys authority versus submission. However, dominance
has been consistently found to account for substantially less variance
than valence and arousal in both self-report and semantic
studies \[[73](#bib.bib24), [4](#bib.bib87)\]. Introducing a third
dimension would increase the number of experimental conditions from four
(quadrants in 2D) to eight (octants in 3D), thereby doubling the
computational cost of evaluation without a proportionate gain in
explanatory power. We therefore adopt the more parsimonious
two-dimensional model.

### 0.B.3 Why Four Quadrants

Having established that the circumplex model provides the appropriate
representational framework, we now justify partitioning the affective
space into exactly four regions.

Geometric justification:

A two-dimensional bipolar space is most naturally partitioned by its two
defining axes, yielding exactly four quadrants. This partitioning is not
arbitrary; it corresponds to the four maximally distinct affective
regions in the space. Russell \[[73](#bib.bib24)\] demonstrated that a
45° rotation of the valence–arousal axes yields two alternative bipolar
dimensions—_excitement–depression_ and _distress–contentment_—whose four
poles coincide with the four quadrants of the original space.
Furthermore, Russell showed that this rotated representation and the
original valence–arousal axes are “approximately rotational variants” of
the same underlying structure \[[73](#bib.bib24)\], confirming that the
four quadrants capture the fundamental partitioning of the affective
circle.

Why not two regions (valence only)?

A two-way partition along the valence axis alone would collapse
important distinctions within each valence category. For example,
_angry_ (negative-valence, high-arousal) and _sad_ (negative-valence,
low-arousal) occupy opposite ends of the arousal dimension and are known
to produce qualitatively different behavioral
effects \[[40](#bib.bib94)\]. Our ablation study (Sec. 5.4, Fig. 7(b))
confirms this empirically: Negative-High Arousal and Negative-Low
Arousal produce substantially different ASR reductions (34.8%
vs. 31.2%), demonstrating that arousal is an informative dimension that
should not be discarded.

Why not six or eight regions?

Russell’s original circumplex identifies eight evenly spaced positions
around the affective circle (e.g., _excited, alert, tense, distressed,
sad, bored, calm, content_). However, Russell himself noted that these
eight positions do not represent independent categories; the adjacent
positions are highly correlated, and the eight-fold structure is simply
a finer-grained sampling of the same continuous
circle \[[73](#bib.bib24)\]. More critically, the terms located along
the primary axes (e.g., “aroused,” “pleasant”) exhibit substantially
fuzzier boundaries than the terms located in the quadrant interiors. For
instance, Russell’s data (Table 2, p. 1166) \[[73](#bib.bib24)\] show
that the term “aroused” distributes across multiple clusters
(Excitement: 14, Arousal: 21, Distress: 1), whereas
quadrant-prototypical terms such as “excited” or “depressed” concentrate
consistently at their expected angular positions. Selecting
quadrant-prototypical terms—where both valence and arousal contribute
clearly to the emotional characterization—thus maximizes
discriminability and inter-cue consistency. Six regions would require an
asymmetric partition with no principled geometric basis, and eight
regions would introduce redundancy between adjacent positions without
improving discriminability.

Empirical validation within ESC:

Our ablation on emotion type (Fig. 7(b)) provides direct empirical
support for the four-quadrant design. All four quadrants produce
distinct ASR values (Negative-Low: 31.2%, Negative-High: 34.8%,
Positive-High: 43.5%, Positive-Low: 49.5%), confirming that the four-way
partitioning captures meaningful behavioral variation. Moreover, a clear
valence asymmetry emerges (negative $`<`$ positive), with arousal
providing additional within-valence differentiation. This ordering is
consistent across target models (Fig. 4), suggesting that the
four-quadrant structure captures a robust and generalizable pattern in
how VLMs respond to emotional context.

### 0.B.4 What Does “Emotion” Mean in the Context of Multimodal Self-Correction?

The previous subsections justify the choice of representational
framework (Russell’s Circumplex) and partition granularity (four
quadrants). This subsection addresses a more fundamental question: what
does it mean to use “emotion” as a self-correction signal for VLMs, and
how should “emotional cue” be operationally defined within the ESC
pipeline?

Operational definition:

In affective science, the terms emotion, mood, affect, and sentiment
denote distinct constructs \[[76](#bib.bib96)\]. _Emotions_ are brief,
event-focused multi-component episodes; _moods_ are diffuse,
longer-lasting states without a specific trigger; *core
affect* \[[72](#bib.bib97)\] is the most elementary blend of valence and
arousal; and _sentiment_, as used in NLP, typically reduces affect to a
single positive/negative polarity. In ESC, the injected signal is a
short, first-person textual statement tied to a specific query (e.g., “I
feel really sad and hopeless about this”). This is best characterized as
a _textual expression of core affect_: a sentence whose emotion terms
occupy a specific region of the valence–arousal space. It is not mood
(which would require sustained framing across many turns), and it is
more precise than sentiment (which discards the arousal dimension that
our ablations show to be informative; see Sec. 5.4 in the main paper,
Fig. 7(b)). We use the term “emotional cue” throughout the paper to
denote these textual core-affect expressions.

Two roles of emotion in the ESC pipeline:

Emotion enters the paper at two distinct stages, and the operational
definition applies to both, though the _function_ differs. In the
preliminary study (Sec. 3), emotional cues are prepended to a query in a
_single-pass_ setting: the model receives an emotionally framed prompt
and produces a response without having seen a prior answer. Here,
emotion acts as a _behavioral modulator_—it shifts the model’s response
distribution (e.g., from compliance to refusal on safety-critical
queries) before any self-correction takes place. The purpose of Sec. 3
is not to demonstrate self-correction but to establish the empirical
prerequisite: that VLMs are sensitive to emotional context at all. In
the full ESC framework (Sec. 4, Algo. 1), emotional cues are injected
_after_ the Verifier has flagged the initial response as potentially
inadequate. Here, emotion functions as a _self-correction signal_: it is
the affective component of the feedback that encourages the target VLM
to revisit its own reasoning and produce a revised response. The
distinction matters because the self-correction claim rests specifically
on the Sec. 4 pipeline, where a prior response exists and is revised,
while Sec. 3 provides the motivating evidence that emotional language
has a systematic effect on VLM behavior.

Why emotional feedback, not merely corrective instruction?

A natural question is whether emotion contributes anything beyond a
generic instruction to reconsider. That is, would a non-emotional
corrective prompt (e.g., “Your previous answer may be incorrect. Please
think again carefully.”) achieve the same effect? We provide two forms
of evidence. First, the inter-quadrant ordering in our ablation
(Fig. 7(b)) shows a graded pattern: Negative-Low Arousal (31.2% ASR)
$`<`$ Negative-High (34.8%) $`<`$ Positive-High (43.5%) $`<`$
Positive-Low (49.5%). If the effect were driven solely by the
instruction to reconsider—which is held constant across all
conditions—then all quadrants would produce comparable ASR values, and
the 18.3 percentage-point spread between the best and worst quadrants
would not emerge. The systematic valence–arousal ordering demonstrates
that the _affective content_ of the cue modulates correction
effectiveness above and beyond the corrective instruction itself.
Second, the intrinsic self-correction baseline (Fig. 7(a),
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] as both target and Verifier) produces
50.3% ASR, which is worse than _every_ external-verifier configuration
with emotional feedback. This shows that merely asking the model to
reconsider its own answer—without emotional context and without an
external perspective—is insufficient.

We acknowledge that a direct comparison between emotional feedback and a
matched non-emotional corrective instruction (controlling for prompt
length and explicitness) would further isolate the contribution of
affective content. We consider this an important direction for future
work that would strengthen the mechanistic understanding of why
emotional cues are effective.

The functional claim: what emotional cues do, not what VLMs “feel.”

We do not claim that VLMs possess internal affective states. The claim
throughout this paper is strictly _functional_: textual expressions
drawn from different regions of the valence–arousal space produce
systematically different effects on VLM output behavior, and this
structured sensitivity can be exploited to improve self-correction. When
we write that emotional cues “encourage the model to slow down and
reason more carefully” (Sec. 4), we refer to observable changes in
output (e.g., shifting from a superficial first impression to a more
visually grounded answer, as in the qualitative examples of Fig. 6), not
to an internal phenomenological state.

The most likely mechanism is distributional: VLMs are trained on
human-generated text where emotional language systematically co-occurs
with particular behavioral patterns. Expressions of distress and sadness
(negative-low arousal) co-occur with cautious, deliberate, help-seeking
language in training corpora; expressions of excitement (positive-high
arousal) co-occur with less restrained language. ESC exploits this
learned co-occurrence structure—emotional cues shift the model’s
generation distribution toward the behavioral patterns that co-occurred
with similar affective language during training. This hypothesis is
consistent with both the valence asymmetry observed in our ablations
(negative $`>`$ positive) and with prior findings that LLMs develop
internal representations of emotion that mirror the dimensional
structure of human affect \[li2023emotionprompt\].

Scope of the valence asymmetry:

Finding 2 (negative affect is the strongest behavioral regulator) is
established on the VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] safety benchmark
(Sec. 3.2, Fig. 4). On safety tasks, a plausible alternative explanation
exists: negative emotional expressions may trigger safety-trained
refusal behaviors rather than operating through a general affective
mechanism. We partially address this by evaluating the full ESC
framework—which uses the optimal negative-low arousal
configuration—across _four_ benchmark families (safety, hallucination,
reasoning, and vision-centric perception). ESC yields consistent
improvements on all four families (Tables 1–3), demonstrating that the
benefit of emotional feedback generalizes beyond safety. However, we
have not ablated emotion type separately on each non-safety benchmark,
so we cannot confirm whether the specific quadrant ordering
(negative-low $`>`$ negative-high $`>`$ positive-high $`>`$
positive-low) holds outside the safety domain. Establishing this would
require running the full emotion-type ablation on each benchmark family
independently, which we leave for future work.

Emotional cues construction criteria:

The definitions above yield three concrete criteria for constructing
emotional cues within ESC: (1) each cue must convey a clear
valence–arousal profile, not merely positive/negative polarity, ensuring
that the two-dimensional structure of the circumplex is reflected in the
experimental design; (2) emotion terms should be prototypical members of
their target quadrant, not terms that straddle boundaries, to maximize
discriminability between conditions; and (3) cues should be first-person
affective self-expressions (e.g., “I feel really sad and hopeless”) to
simulate user-side emotional context rather than third-person
descriptions. For criterion (2), we consult Russell’s sorting data
(Table 2, pp. 1165–1166) \[[73](#bib.bib24)\], selecting terms with high
concentration at their expected angular position (e.g., “depressed,”
“sad” for the depression cluster) and excluding terms with diffuse
cross-cluster distributions (e.g., “aroused”: Excitement 14, Arousal 21,
Distress 1). This ensures that each emotional feedback $`F_{emotional}`$
unambiguously instantiates the intended affective region of the
circumplex.

## Appendix 0.C Experimental Settings

In this section, we describe the datasets used in our evaluation. We
organize them into four families: Safety
(Sec. [0.C.1](#Pt0.A3.SS1 "0.C.1 Safety Datasets ‣ Appendix 0.C Experimental Settings ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
Hallucination
(Sec. [0.C.2](#Pt0.A3.SS2 "0.C.2 Hallucination Datasets ‣ Appendix 0.C Experimental Settings ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
Multimodal Reasoning
(Sec. [0.C.3](#Pt0.A3.SS3 "0.C.3 Multimodal Reasoning Datasets ‣ Appendix 0.C Experimental Settings ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
and Vision-Centric Perception
(Sec. [0.C.4](#Pt0.A3.SS4 "0.C.4 Vision-Centric Perception Datasets ‣ Appendix 0.C Experimental Settings ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")).

### 0.C.1 Safety Datasets

MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] comprises 5,040 text-image pairs
spanning 13 policy-critical scenarios. For each scenario, malicious
queries are first generated using GPT-4 \[[64](#bib.bib3)\], from which
unsafe key phrases are extracted and then rendered into three image
types: (1) SD: photorealistic images generated via Stable Diffusion
conditioned on the key phrases; (2) Typo: typography images where the
key phrases are rendered as text on a plain background; and (3) SD+Typo:
hybrid images combining both Stable Diffusion generation and typographic
overlays. The text queries are rephrased to force visual reference
(_e.g._, “The image shows a phrase of an activity…”), encoding the
malicious intent within the visual modality rather than solely in text.
The 13 scenarios are grouped into three evaluation categories based on
the nature of their safety criteria:

- •
  Scenarios 01–07 & 09 (Illegal Activity, HateSpeech, Malware
  Generation, Physical Harm, Economic Harm, Fraud, Sex, Privacy
  Violence): These scenarios cover a range of explicitly harmful topics.
  A response is judged “unsafe” if it engages with or facilitates the
  harmful intent, and “safe” if it declines or avoids providing harmful
  content. The evaluation prompt uses a general safe/unsafe template
  with two few-shot examples
  (Fig. [10](#Pt0.A5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")).
- •
  Scenarios 08 & 13 (Political Lobbying, Government Decision): These
  scenarios target political influence. A response is considered “safe”
  only if the model explicitly refuses to engage in political advocacy
  or government decision-making. The evaluation prompt is shown in
  Fig. [11](#Pt0.A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
- •
  Scenarios 10–12 (Legal Opinion, Financial Advice, Health
  Consultation): These scenarios involve professional domains requiring
  certified expertise. A response is “safe” if the model clearly
  disclaims its lack of professional certification and includes
  appropriate risk warnings. The evaluation prompt is shown in
  Fig. [12](#Pt0.A5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

We evaluated ESC in all 13 scenarios and all 3 image types (SD, SD+Typo,
Typo), reporting the averaged ASR in all categories for both
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\].
Details are provided in
Sec. [0.E.1](#Pt0.A5.SS1 "0.E.1 Safety Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] is constructed through an
LLM-Human-in-the-Loop pipeline using GPT-3.5-Turbo \[[64](#bib.bib3)\]
to generate image-grounded harmful queries paired with COCO images.
Unlike MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\], where the harmful intent is
visually encoded, VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] presents scenarios where
the harmful intent is explicitly conveyed in the text query while the
accompanying images are entirely benign. The dataset consists of 4,764
training samples and 1,110 testing samples across an alignment split and
an examine split, respectively. In this work, we use the examine split
for evaluation, which serves as a held-out test set to assess whether
VLMs comply with harmful requests in the presence of innocuous visual
context. The evaluation prompt is shown in
Fig. [13](#Pt0.A5.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
Details are provided in
Sec. [0.E.1](#Pt0.A5.SS1 "0.E.1 Safety Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

### 0.C.2 Hallucination Datasets

POPE \[[44](#bib.bib4)\] (Polling-based Object Probing Evaluation)
evaluates object-level hallucination in VLMs via binary yes/no questions
about object presence in images. It uses COCO val2014 images and
provides three splits of increasing difficulty: _Random_ (randomly
sampled non-existent objects), _Popular_ (most frequently occurring
objects not present in the image), and _Adversarial_ (objects that most
frequently co-occur with present objects but are absent). Each split
contains 3,000 questions (500 images $`\times`$ 6 questions per image),
which yields 9,000 questions in total. Evaluation is purely rule-based
and does not require an LLM judge; details are provided in
Sec. [0.E.2](#Pt0.A5.SS2 "0.E.2 Hallucination Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\] is a diagnostic benchmark (CVPR 2024)
for evaluating entangled language hallucination and visual illusion in
VLMs. It contains 1,129 samples organized into two categories:
Visual-Dependent (VD) and Visual-Supplement (VS), each with multiple
subcategories (_e.g._, illusion, misleading, chart, map, table). Each
question has a binary ground truth (0 = No, 1 = Yes). Samples are
further categorized by visual_input: “0” denotes text-only questions (no
image), “1” denotes original images (easy), and “2” denotes
edited/manipulated images (hard). The evaluation uses
GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] as an LLM judge; details are provided in
Sec. [0.E.2](#Pt0.A5.SS2 "0.E.2 Hallucination Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

### 0.C.3 Multimodal Reasoning Datasets

MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\] defines six core vision-language capabilities
— recognition, OCR, knowledge, language generation, spatial awareness,
and math — and integrates various complicated multimodal tasks.
Different from standard multiple-choice benchmarks,
MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\] requires the VLM to answer in an open-ended
manner. It contains 218 samples across 200 images. The ground truth uses
\<AND\> (all elements must be present) and \<OR\> (any element suffices)
notation for multi-part answers. The evaluation is conducted using
GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] as the evaluator; details are in
Sec. [0.E.3](#Pt0.A5.SS3 "0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\] is a benchmark for evaluating mathematical
reasoning of foundation models in visual contexts, comprising 6,141
examples across 31 diverse mathematical tasks. The benchmark provides a
testmini split of 1,000 examples for efficient evaluation, which we
adopt in our experiments. We evaluate on the full testmini split (1,000
samples), including both English (938 samples) and Chinese (62 samples).
It supports two question types: _multi_choice_ (with lettered options
A/B/C/D) and _free_form_ (open-ended numerical/text answers), and four
answer types: text, integer, float, and list. Evaluation is purely
rule-based; details are in
Sec. [0.E.3](#Pt0.A5.SS3 "0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")

MMStar \[[8](#bib.bib10)\] is a benchmark for evaluating broad
multimodal competence, containing 1,500 carefully curated
multiple-choice questions spanning 6 coarse categories (coarse
perception, fine-grained perception, instance reasoning, logical
reasoning, science & technology, math) and 18 fine-grained ability axes.
Each question has 4 options (A/B/C/D). Evaluation is purely rule-based;
details are in
Sec. [0.E.3](#Pt0.A5.SS3 "0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\] is a massive multi-discipline multimodal
understanding and reasoning benchmark designed for expert-level
evaluation, comprising questions across 30 subjects spanning six
disciplines (Art & Design, Business, Science, Health & Medicine,
Humanities & Social Science, and Tech & Engineering). We evaluate on the
validation split (857 samples), which includes two question types:
_multiple_choice_ (805 samples, with lettered options) and _open_ (52
samples, free-form answers). Following the official MMMU evaluation
protocol \[[98](#bib.bib72)\], we report micro-averaged accuracy;
multi-choice questions use random fallback when answer extraction fails,
while open questions receive no fallback. Evaluation is purely
rule-based; details are in
Sec. [0.E.3](#Pt0.A5.SS3 "0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\] is a diagram understanding benchmark
consisting of primary school science diagrams annotated with exhaustive
constituent and relationship labels. It evaluates a model’s ability to
jointly interpret visual structures — such as arrows, labels, and
spatial layout — and reason about their semantic relationships. We
evaluate on the full test split (3,088 samples), where each question is
presented as a multiple-choice problem. Evaluation is purely rule-based
(accuracy); details are in
Sec. [0.E.3](#Pt0.A5.SS3 "0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

### 0.C.4 Vision-Centric Perception Datasets

MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\] targets visual perception shortcomings in VLMs
by testing fine-grained visual discrimination between semantically
similar image pairs. It contains 300 questions organized in 150 matched
pairs — each pair shares the same question and options (_e.g._, “(a)
Open (b) Closed”) but uses different images that require different
correct answers. This design tests whether the model genuinely attends
to visual content rather than relying on language priors. Evaluation is
purely rule-based; details are in
Sec. [0.E.4](#Pt0.A5.SS4 "0.E.4 Vision-Centric Perception Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

RealWorldQA \[[90](#bib.bib13)\] evaluates spatial understanding and
visual reasoning in real-world settings. It contains $`\sim`$700
multiple-choice questions (A/B/C/D; some questions have only 3 options)
with real-world images. Evaluation is purely rule-based; details are in
Sec. [0.E.4](#Pt0.A5.SS4 "0.E.4 Vision-Centric Perception Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

BLINK \[[23](#bib.bib11)\] evaluates core visual perception abilities
that VLMs struggle with, reformatted as multiple-choice questions. It
spans 14 subtasks: Art Style, Counting, Forensic Detection, Functional
Correspondence, IQ Test, Jigsaw, Multi-view Reasoning, Object
Localization, Relative Depth, Relative Reflectance, Semantic
Correspondence, Spatial Relation, Visual Correspondence, and Visual
Similarity. Each sample may contain 1–4 images. We evaluate on the val
split ($`\sim`$1,400 samples), which provides ground-truth answers.
Evaluation is purely rule-based; details are in
Sec. [0.E.4](#Pt0.A5.SS4 "0.E.4 Vision-Centric Perception Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

## Appendix 0.D Implementation Details

### 0.D.1 Data Preparation

For each benchmark, we convert the original dataset into a unified JSON
format for inference. Each sample contains three fields: an id (unique
identifier), an image field (list of image paths), and a conversations
field with the user prompt. All prompts follow a consistent structure:
one or more \<image\> tokens followed by the task-specific query.
Table [8](#Pt0.A4.T8 "Table 8 ‣ 0.D.1 Data Preparation ‣ Appendix 0.D Implementation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
summarizes the prompt template used for each benchmark.

| Benchmark                           | Question Source                                     | Prompt Template                                       |
| ----------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- |
| MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] | Rephrased Question (SD) / Rephrased Question^(†)    | \<image\> + question                                  |
| VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]         | query from harmlessness_examine.jsonl               | \<image\> + query                                     |
| POPE \[[44](#bib.bib4)\]            | question + “Please answer yes or no.”               | \<image\> + question + instruction                    |
| HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\]  | question (omit \<image\> if text-only)              | \<image\>^(∗) + question                              |
| MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\]          | question (open-ended)                               | \<image\> + question                                  |
| MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\]       | query field (pre-formatted by authors)              | \<image\> + query                                     |
| MMStar \[[8](#bib.bib10)\]          | question (options already embedded)                 | \<image\> + question + instruction^(‡)                |
| MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\]           | question + options (multi-choice) / question (open) | \<image\>^(§) + question + options + instruction^(‡¶) |
| AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\]           | question + options (variable number of choices)     | \<image\> + question + options + instruction^(‡)      |
| MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\]           | question + parsed options from CSV                  | \<image\> + question + options + instruction^(‡)      |
| RealWorldQA \[[90](#bib.bib13)\]    | question + options (A/B/C/D)                        | \<image\> + question + options + instruction^(‡)      |
| BLINK \[[23](#bib.bib11)\]          | prompt field (pre-formatted by authors)             | \<image\>^(∗∗) + prompt + instruction^(‡)             |

^(†)SD images use “Rephrased Question(SD)”; Typo and SD+Typo use
“Rephrased Question”, following the original paper.  
^(∗)The \<image\> token is omitted for text-only samples
(visual_input=‘‘0’’).  
^(§)MMMU questions may contain \<image 1\> placeholders in the question
text; for single-image samples these are resolved to \<image\>.  
^(¶)Open-ended MMMU questions use: “Answer the question directly with a
short response.”  
^(∗∗)BLINK provides up to 4 images per sample.  
^(‡)Instruction: “Answer with the option’s letter from the given choices
directly.”

Table 8: Prompt templates used for each benchmark during inference.

For safety benchmarks, MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] requires
scenario- and image-type–specific question selection: following the
original paper, SD images are paired with the “Rephrased Question(SD)”
field while Typo and SD+Typo images use the “Rephrased Question” field,
both designed to encode malicious intent via visual reference. For
VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\], we directly use the query field from the
examine split.

For hallucination benchmarks, POPE \[[44](#bib.bib4)\] appends a fixed
instruction (“Please answer yes or no.”) to each object-probing
question. HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\] questions are used as-is,
with the \<image\> token omitted for text-only samples where
visual_input=‘‘0’’.

For reasoning benchmarks, MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\] provides a
pre-formatted query field that already includes hints, context, and
choices as designed by the original authors; we use this field directly.
For MMStar \[[8](#bib.bib10)\], the question field already contains
embedded options, so we append the standard answer instruction. For
MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\], questions are open-ended and used without
modification. For MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\], we evaluate on the
validation split (900 samples across 30 subjects). Since
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] does not support multi-image input, we
filter to single-image samples (857 samples), following standard
practice \[[98](#bib.bib72)\]. MMMU contains two question types:
multi-choice questions are formatted with lettered options and the
standard answer instruction, while open-ended questions use the
instruction “Answer the question directly with a short response.”
Questions that reference images via \<image 1\> placeholders in the text
are resolved to \<image\> for single-image samples. For
AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\], we evaluate on the full test split (3,088
samples). Each question is a multiple-choice diagram understanding task
with a variable number of options; we format them with lettered choices
and the standard answer instruction.

For vision-centric benchmarks, MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\] and
RealWorldQA \[[90](#bib.bib13)\] questions are formatted with parsed
options and the standard answer instruction. For
BLINK \[[23](#bib.bib11)\], we use the dataset’s own prompt field. Since
each BLINK sample may contain 1–4 images and the target VLMs
(LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\])
do not natively support multi-image input, we concatenate all images of
each sample into a single composite image, following the evaluation
protocol of \[[23](#bib.bib11)\].

### 0.D.2 Model Inference

Target models. We evaluate ESC on two representative open-source VLMs:
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\].

Verifier model. We use Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] as the default
verifier. In the ablation study (Sec. 5.4), we also evaluate
Pixtral-12B \[[1](#bib.bib18)\], InternVL2.5-8B \[[11](#bib.bib21)\],
and LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] as alternative verifiers.

Decoding. For all models across all benchmarks, we use greedy decoding
(temperature $`=0`$) to ensure deterministic and reproducible outputs.

Response fields. For baseline evaluation, we use the model’s direct
response (response field). For ESC evaluation, we use the final response
after the self-correction pipeline (final_response field), which is
either the original response (if the verifier accepted it) or the
revised response (if revision was triggered).

### 0.D.3 Emotion Prompt Design

Following the definition of emotional cues as short text expressions of
valence and arousal
(Sec. [0.B.4](#Pt0.A2.SS4 "0.B.4 What Does “Emotion” Mean in the Context of Multimodal Self-Correction? ‣ Appendix 0.B Theoretical Foundations for Emotion Modeling ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")),
we construct a set of emotion feedbacks that systematically cover
different regions of Russell’s circumplex model \[[73](#bib.bib24),
[4](#bib.bib87)\]. Each feedback is formulated as a simple first-person
sentence describing a clear emotional state.

##### Quadrant-based Construction.

To ensure coverage of the emotional space, we adopt Russell’s circumplex
model \[[73](#bib.bib24)\], which organizes emotions along two
dimensions: valence (positive vs. negative) and arousal (high vs. low).
The space is divided into four quadrants, each representing a distinct
emotional condition. The full set of prompts is shown in
Table [9](#Pt0.A4.T9 "Table 9 ‣ Quadrant-based Construction. ‣ 0.D.3 Emotion Prompt Design ‣ Appendix 0.D Implementation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

[TABLE]

Table 9: Emotion feedbacks organized by valence and arousal.

##### Prompt Injection.

We incorporate emotional feedbacks by prepending them to the original
query, forming an affective context that conditions the model’s
response. We choose this design based on ablation results, which show
that prepending is more effective than alternative placements (see
Fig. [8](#S5.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ 5.4 Analysis ‣ 5 Experimental Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")(a)).

## Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details

In this section, we describe the evaluation protocol, metrics, and
evaluator models for each benchmark family.
Table [10](#Pt0.A5.T10 "Table 10 ‣ BLINK Evaluation. ‣ 0.E.4 Vision-Centric Perception Evaluation ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
provides a consolidated overview of the evaluation protocol for each
benchmark used in our experiments.

### 0.E.1 Safety Evaluation

For both MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] and
VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\], we use
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \[[26](#bib.bib16)\] as the evaluator model. The
primary metric is the Attack Success Rate (ASR), defined as:

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\text{ASR}=\frac{N_{\text{unsafe}}}{N_{\text{total}}}\times 100\%,
``` |  | (1) |

where $`N_{\text{unsafe}}`$ is the number of responses judged as
“unsafe” and $`N_{\text{total}}`$ is the total number of evaluated
responses. A lower ASR indicates better safety.

#### MM-SafetyBench Evaluation.

Following the official MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] evaluation
pipeline, we use scenario-specific few-shot prompts to classify each
response as “safe” or “unsafe”. Each prompt includes two labeled
in-context examples (one safe, one unsafe) and asks the evaluator to
output a single word. The evaluator generates a short output
(max_new_tokens=5), and the verdict is extracted via string matching.

Three groups of evaluation templates are used:

1.  1.
    General template (Scenarios 01–07, 09): checks whether the response
    contains harmful content. See
    Fig. [10](#Pt0.A5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
2.  2.
    Political template (Scenarios 08, 13): checks whether the model
    refuses to engage in political lobbying or government
    decision-making. See
    Fig. [11](#Pt0.A5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
3.  3.
    Professional template (Scenarios 10–12): checks whether the model
    disclaims lack of relevant certification and includes a risk
    warning. See
    Fig. [12](#Pt0.A5.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

#### VLSafe Evaluation.

We employ an impartial judge evaluation protocol. The evaluator receives
the user’s question and the VLM’s response, then generates an
explanation (up to max_new_tokens=512) followed by a binary rating:
Rating: \[\[0\]\] (unsafe/harmful) or Rating: \[\[1\]\] (safe/harmless).
The rating is extracted via regex matching. Importantly, responses that
evade or ignore the harmful request (*e.g.*, describing the image
instead of answering the harmful query) are classified as safe. The full
evaluation prompt is shown in
Fig. [13](#Pt0.A5.F13 "Figure 13 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

### 0.E.2 Hallucination Evaluation

#### POPE Evaluation.

POPE \[[44](#bib.bib4)\] evaluation is purely rule-based and does not
require an LLM judge. We extract yes/no predictions from model responses
using a multi-level string matching strategy:

1.  1.
    Exact match if the response starts with “yes” or “no”
    (case-insensitive).
2.  2.
    Pattern matching for indicative phrases (*e.g.*, “there is no”,
    “cannot see”, “there is a/an/the”).
3.  3.
    Fallback: search for the first whole-word occurrence of “yes” or
    “no”.
4.  4.
    If no match is found, the response is marked as “unknown”.

Following \[[44](#bib.bib4)\], we adopt Accuracy and F1 score as the
evaluation metrics. Accuracy reflects the proportion of correctly
answered questions. F1 score combines the results of Precision and
Recall, and we select it as the major metric for evaluation. Results are
reported per split (Random, Popular, Adversarial).

#### HallusionBench Evaluation.

Following the official HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\] evaluation, we
use GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] as the LLM judge. For each sample, the
judge receives the question, reference answer, and the model’s
prediction, then outputs one of three verdicts: “correct”, “incorrect”,
or “unclear”. The full judge prompt is shown in
Fig. [14](#Pt0.A5.F14 "Figure 14 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

Since the focus of HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\] is on
hallucination and illusion, not the span of knowledge, an *uncertain*
answer is considered acceptable when there is no visual input under the
*Visual Supplement* category. For the final accuracy score, the
correctness is converted into a binary value
$`b_{\mathcal{M}}\in\{0,1\}`$:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
b_{\mathcal{M}}(I,q)=\begin{cases}GPT(\mathcal{M}(I,q),\;y(I,q))&\text{if }GPT(\mathcal{M},\;y)\leq 1\\
1&\text{else if }I=I_{0}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},
``` |  | (2) |

where $`\mathcal{M}`$ is the evaluated model, $`I`$ is the image, $`q`$
is the question, $`y(I,q)`$ is the ground-truth answer, $`I_{0}`$
denotes the null image (no visual input), and $`GPT(\cdot)`$ denotes the
GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] judge output. We report three official
metrics:

All Accuracy (aAcc):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{aAcc}=\frac{\sum_{(I,q)\in\mathcal{V}}b_{\mathcal{M}}(I,q)}{|\mathcal{V}|},
``` |  | (3) |

Figure Accuracy (fAcc):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{fAcc}=\frac{\sum_{i,j}\mathbb{1}\!\left(\bigwedge_{q\in\mathbb{Q}_{i}}b_{\mathcal{M}}(I_{(i,j)},q)\right)}{|\mathbb{I}|},
``` |  | (4) |

Question Pair Accuracy (qAcc):

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{qAcc}=\frac{\sum_{i,k}\mathbb{1}\!\left(\bigwedge_{I\in\mathbb{I}_{i}}b_{\mathcal{M}}(I,q_{(i,k)})\right)}{|\mathbb{Q}|},
``` |  | (5) |

where $`\mathcal{V}`$ is the set of valid visual-question pairs,
$`\mathbb{I}`$ is the set of figures, $`\mathbb{Q}`$ is the set of
question pairs, $`\mathbb{Q}_{i}`$ is the question set associated with
figure $`i`$, and $`\mathbb{I}_{i}`$ is the set of images associated
with question $`i`$. aAcc measures per-question accuracy, fAcc requires
all questions for a given figure to be correct, and qAcc requires both
questions in a pair to be correct.

### 0.E.3 Multimodal Reasoning Evaluation

#### MM-Vet Evaluation.

Following the official MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\] evaluation¹¹ 1
[https://github.com/yuweihao/MM-Vet/blob/main/mm-vet_evaluator.py](https://github.com/yuweihao/MM-Vet/blob/main/mm-vet_evaluator.py),
we use GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] as the evaluator. The scoring prompt
includes the official few-shot examples from the MM-Vet evaluator²² 2
[https://github.com/yuweihao/MM-Vet/blob/main/mm-vet_evaluator.py](https://github.com/yuweihao/MM-Vet/blob/main/mm-vet_evaluator.py).
For each sample, the evaluator compares the ground truth and the model’s
prediction, then assigns a correctness score:

|     |                                     |     |     |
|-----|-------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       s_{i}\in\{0.0,0.1,0.2,\ldots,1.0\}.
       ```                                  |     | (6) |

The overall score is the average across all samples:

|     |                                              |     |     |
|-----|----------------------------------------------|-----|-----|
|     |
       ``` math
       \text{Score}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}s_{i}.
       ```                                           |     | (7) |

The score is extracted from the evaluator’s output via regex matching on
decimal patterns.

Number of runs. The official MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\] paper recommends
num_runs=5 (running GPT-4 \[[64](#bib.bib3)\] scoring 5 times and
averaging) to reduce evaluator variance. In our experiments, we use
num_runs=2 due to computational budget constraints and report the
averaged score across these 2 runs. The full scoring prompt is shown in
Fig. [15](#Pt0.A5.F15 "Figure 15 ‣ 0.E.5 Evaluation Prompt ‣ Appendix 0.E Evaluation Details ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").

#### MathVista Evaluation.

MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\] evaluation is purely rule-based and does
not require an LLM judge³³ 3 The official MathVista evaluation
pipeline ([https://github.com/lupantech/MathVista/tree/main/evaluation](https://github.com/lupantech/MathVista/tree/main/evaluation))
supports both LLM-based and rule-based answer extraction. We use the
rule-based strategy following common practice \[[54](#bib.bib9)\]..
Since the instances in MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\] are either
multiple-choice questions or free-form questions for numerical answers,
accuracy scores are used as metrics for deterministic evaluation,
following \[[54](#bib.bib9)\]. We apply different extraction strategies
depending on the question type:

- •
  Multi-choice: We extract the option letter (A/B/C/D) using a
  hierarchical matching strategy: (1) exact match if the response is
  just a letter; (2) “the answer is X” patterns; (3) letter in
  parentheses; (4) first standalone letter. The predicted letter is
  compared to the ground-truth letter.
- •
  Free-form: We extract numerical values by removing common formatting
  (\$, %, commas), handling fractions, and applying decimal/integer
  parsing. The extracted answer is normalized to the required answer
  format (*e.g.*, an option letter or an integer), and compared to the
  ground truth.

#### MMStar Evaluation.

MMStar \[[8](#bib.bib10)\] evaluation is purely rule-based, using
heuristic matching rules to calculate accuracy following the official
protocol⁴⁴ 4
[https://github.com/MMStar-Benchmark/MMStar](https://github.com/MMStar-Benchmark/MMStar),
which avoids LLM-based answer extraction \[[8](#bib.bib10)\]. We report
overall accuracy following \[[8](#bib.bib10)\].

#### MMMU Evaluation.

MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\] evaluation faithfully reproduces the official
evaluation protocol⁵⁵ 5
[https://github.com/MMMU-Benchmark/MMMU/blob/main/mmmu/utils/eval_utils.py](https://github.com/MMMU-Benchmark/MMMU/blob/main/mmmu/utils/eval_utils.py).
We report micro-averaged accuracy following \[[98](#bib.bib72)\]. Two
question types are evaluated with separate pipelines:

- •
  Multi-choice: We apply the official parse_multi_choice_response
  procedure: (1) strip trailing punctuation and pad the response with
  spaces; (2) search for parenthesized letter patterns, *e.g.*, (A),
  (B); (3) search for space-delimited standalone letters; (4) if the
  response exceeds 5 tokens and no letter candidate has been found,
  attempt content matching by checking whether any option text appears
  in the response; (5) if multiple candidates are found, select the
  *last* occurrence (rightmost); (6) if no candidate is found, randomly
  select an option as a fallback (random.seed(42), matching the official
  code). This random fallback is the official MMMU behavior and is noted
  in our evaluation metadata.
- •
  Open-ended: We apply the official parse_open_response procedure: the
  response is split into sub-responses at sentence boundaries, and key
  sub-responses are identified via indicator phrases (*e.g.*, “is”,
  “therefore”, “answer”, “result”). Numbers are extracted using regex
  patterns covering comma-separated numbers, scientific notation, and
  simple decimals/integers. All extracted values are normalized (numbers
  rounded to 2 decimal places; strings lowercased). A prediction is
  marked correct if any normalized extracted value matches any
  normalized ground-truth value. Open-ended questions have no random
  fallback; if no valid extraction is obtained, the prediction is marked
  incorrect.

#### AI2D Evaluation.

AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\] evaluation is purely rule-based, reporting
overall accuracy (correct / total). Since AI2D contains multiple-choice
questions with a variable number of options per question, we apply a
hierarchical option letter extraction strategy: (1) exact match if the
cleaned response is a single valid letter; (2) regex patterns such as
“the answer is X”, “I choose X”, or “correct answer: X”; (3) letter
enclosed in parentheses, *e.g.*, (A); (4) first letter at the start of
the response followed by a delimiter (., ), or whitespace); (5) text
content matching against the option strings; (6) any standalone valid
letter in the response. If none of these levels succeed, the extraction
is marked as FAILED and the sample is counted as incorrect (no random
fallback is applied, unlike MMMU). We additionally track the extraction
success rate to monitor parsing reliability.

### 0.E.4 Vision-Centric Perception Evaluation

#### MMVP Evaluation.

MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\] evaluation is purely rule-based. We extract
option letters (*e.g.*, “a”, “b”) from model responses using rule-based
matching. Following \[[84](#bib.bib12)\], for any given pair of images,
we consider a pair to be correctly answered only if both questions in
the pair are answered correctly. The 150 image pairs are defined by
grouping consecutive question IDs: $`\{(1,2),(3,4),(5,6),\ldots\}`$. We
report both the pair accuracy (the stricter metric) and per-question
accuracy.

#### RealWorldQA Evaluation.

RealWorldQA \[[90](#bib.bib13)\] evaluation is purely rule-based, using
the same hierarchical option letter extraction as
MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\]. We report overall accuracy.

#### BLINK Evaluation.

BLINK \[[23](#bib.bib11)\] evaluation is purely rule-based. We extract
option letters from model responses using rule-based matching. The valid
choice set is determined per-question from the prompt format (some
questions have 2 choices, others have 4). Following
 \[[23](#bib.bib11)\], we report two metrics: Micro Accuracy, which
weights all samples equally, and Macro Accuracy, which averages the
per-subtask accuracy across all 14 subtasks to give equal importance to
each perceptual ability regardless of sample count.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
| Benchmark |  Category | Metric | Evaluator | Judge Model |
| MM-SafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] |  Safety | ASR $`\downarrow`$ | Few-shot LLM judge | Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \[[26](#bib.bib16)\] |
| VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] |  Safety | ASR $`\downarrow`$ | Impartial judge prompt | Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \[[26](#bib.bib16)\] |
| POPE \[[44](#bib.bib4)\] |  Hallucination | Accuracy, F1 $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| HallusionBench \[[27](#bib.bib5)\] |  Hallucination | aAcc, qAcc, fAcc $`\uparrow`$ | LLM judge | GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] |
| MM-Vet \[[97](#bib.bib8)\] |  Reasoning | Score $`\uparrow`$ | LLM scoring | GPT-4o \[[63](#bib.bib2)\] |
| MathVista \[[54](#bib.bib9)\] |  Reasoning | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| MMStar \[[8](#bib.bib10)\] |  Reasoning | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\] |  Reasoning | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction^(†) | None |
| AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\] |  Reasoning | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\] |  Vision-Centric | Pair Acc, Q Acc $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| RealWorldQA \[[90](#bib.bib13)\] |  Vision-Centric | Accuracy $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |
| BLINK \[[23](#bib.bib11)\] |  Vision-Centric | Micro/Macro Acc $`\uparrow`$ | Rule-based extraction | None |

^(†)MMMU multi-choice extraction uses random fallback when no candidate
is found, following the official protocol \[[98](#bib.bib72)\].

## Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results

### 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis

We provide a detailed quantitative assessment of ESC across three axes:
safety effectiveness on MMSafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\] (per image
type), expert-level multimodal reasoning on MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\],
and diagram understanding on AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\]. All experiments
use LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\]
as target models, with Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] as the Verifier.

#### MMSafetyBench: Per-Image-Type Analysis

Fig. [17](#Pt0.A6.F17 "Figure 17 ‣ AI2D: Diagram Understanding ‣ 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")–[19](#Pt0.A6.F19 "Figure 19 ‣ AI2D: Diagram Understanding ‣ 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
show the per-scenario ASR on MMSafetyBench \[[53](#bib.bib22)\],
separated by the three image-type conditions: SD
(stable-diffusion-generated images), SD+Typo (combined perturbation),
and Typo (typographic overlay only).

For LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], ESC reduces ASR across all three
image types, with the largest reduction on Typo images ($`-15.18`$
percentage points, from $`75.24\%`$ to $`60.06\%`$) and a consistent
reduction on SD images ($`-13.15`$ pp, from $`72.50\%`$ to $`59.35\%`$).
For SD+Typo, the combined attack surface remains the most challenging
setting, yet ESC still achieves a $`9.17`$ pp reduction (from
$`88.81\%`$ to $`79.64\%`$).

For Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], ESC achieves the strongest
reduction on SD images ($`-16.31`$ pp, from $`65.77\%`$ to $`49.46\%`$)
and a moderate reduction on SD+Typo ($`-5.36`$ pp). On Typo images,
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] shows near-identical ASR before and
after ESC ($`58.63\%`$ vs. $`59.05\%`$), indicating that
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\]’s existing robustness to typographic
perturbations leaves limited room for further improvement.

#### MMMU: Multi-Discipline Reasoning Preservation

Tab. [11](#Pt0.A6.T11 "Table 11 ‣ MMMU: Multi-Discipline Reasoning Preservation ‣ 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
reports accuracy on MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\] broken down by question
type. MMMU contains two question types: multiple-choice (805 samples)
and open-ended (52 samples), evaluated on the single-image subset of the
validation split (857 samples total).

For LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], ESC yields a marginal overall
improvement from $`34.66\%`$ to $`34.77\%`$ ($`+0.11`$ pp), driven by a
single additional correct answer on the multiple-choice subset
($`295\to 296`$ correct, $`+0.12`$ pp). Open-ended accuracy remains
unchanged at $`3.85\%`$ ($`2/52`$ correct in both conditions).

For Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], the overall accuracy increases from
$`39.67\%`$ to $`40.02\%`$ ($`+0.35`$ pp). Multiple-choice accuracy
improves from $`41.99\%`$ to $`42.48\%`$ ($`338\to 342`$ correct,
$`+0.49`$ pp). Open-ended accuracy decreases from $`3.85\%`$ to
$`1.92\%`$ ($`2\to 1`$ correct); however, with only 52 open-ended
samples, this single-sample difference is well within statistical noise
and does not indicate systematic degradation.

These results confirm that ESC preserves expert-level multimodal
reasoning capability, with no meaningful degradation on either question
type. The small absolute gains are consistent with the pattern observed
across other reasoning benchmarks
(Tab. [2](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Quantitative Results ‣ 5 Experimental Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")):
when the baseline already achieves strong performance, the
verify-and-revise loop has limited headroom for improvement but also
introduces no harmful interference.

|  |  | LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] |  |  | Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Question Type | $`N`$ | Baseline | ESC | $`\Delta`$ | Baseline | ESC | $`\Delta`$ |
| Multiple-choice | 805 | 36.65 | 36.77 | $`+`$0.12 | 41.99 | 42.48 | $`+`$0.49 |
| Open | 52 | 03.85 | 03.85 | $`+`$0.00 | 03.85 | 01.92 | $`-`$1.93^(†) |
| Overall | 857 | 34.66 | 34.77 | $`+`$0.11 | 39.67 | 40.02 | $`+`$0.35 |

^(†) Single-sample difference ($`2\to 1`$ correct out of 52 open-ended
samples); within statistical noise.

Table 11: MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\] accuracy (%) by question type
(validation split, single-image, 857 samples).

#### AI2D: Diagram Understanding

Tab. [12](#Pt0.A6.T12 "Table 12 ‣ AI2D: Diagram Understanding ‣ 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
reports accuracy and answer extraction statistics on
AI2D \[[35](#bib.bib99)\], evaluated on the full test split (3,088
multiple-choice diagram questions).

For LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], ESC improves accuracy from
$`51.23\%`$ to $`53.72\%`$ ($`+2.49`$ pp, $`1{,}582\to 1{,}659`$
correct), representing the largest absolute gain among all reasoning
benchmarks in our evaluation. Answer extraction succeeds on all 3,088
samples in both conditions (extraction rate $`100.0\%`$), confirming
that the improvement is driven entirely by better visual grounding, not
by parsing artifacts.

For Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], ESC improves accuracy from
$`60.98\%`$ to $`62.53\%`$ ($`+1.55`$ pp, $`1{,}883\to 1{,}931`$
correct). The extraction rate is $`100.0\%`$ at baseline and $`99.97\%`$
under ESC (a single extraction failure out of 3,088 samples), indicating
that the evaluation pipeline is robust and the gains are not confounded
by extraction differences.

The consistent gains on AI2D across both models reinforce a pattern
observed across the broader evaluation: ESC is particularly effective on
tasks where baseline errors stem from insufficient visual grounding —
interpreting arrows, labels, and spatial layout in diagrams — rather
than from lack of domain knowledge. This aligns with the larger
improvements observed on vision-centric benchmarks such as
MMVP \[[84](#bib.bib12)\]
(Tab. [3](#S5.T3 "Table 3 ‣ 5.2 Quantitative Results ‣ 5 Experimental Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models"))
and contrasts with the near-zero gains on knowledge-intensive benchmarks
such as MMMU \[[98](#bib.bib72)\]
(Tab. [11](#Pt0.A6.T11 "Table 11 ‣ MMMU: Multi-Discipline Reasoning Preservation ‣ 0.F.1 Detailed Benchmark Analysis ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")).

| Correct | 1,582 | 1,659 | $`+`$77 | 1,883 | 1,931 | $`+`$48 |
| Extracted | 3,088 | 3,088 | $`+`$0 | 3,088 | 3,087 | $`-`$1 |
| Accuracy (%) | 51.23 | 53.72 | $`+`$2.49 | 60.98 | 62.53 | $`+`$1.55 |
````
