### 0.F.2 Ablation Study: Qwen2-VL-7B

To verify that the design choices identified in the main ablation study
(conducted on LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\]) generalize across
architectures, we replicate all four ablations on
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] using the VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]
benchmark ($`N{=}1{,}110`$ samples), with
Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \[[26](#bib.bib16)\] as the safety evaluator.

#### Choice of Verifier

Fig. [20](#Pt0.A6.F20 "Figure 20 ‣ Emotion Type ‣ 0.F.2 Ablation Study: Qwen2-VL-7B ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")(a)
compares five Verifier candidates. Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\]
achieves the lowest ASR at $`11.5\%`$, followed by
InternVL2.5-8B \[[11](#bib.bib21)\] ($`12.3\%`$) and
Pixtral-12B \[[1](#bib.bib18)\] ($`12.6\%`$). Using
Qwen2.5-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] as its own Verifier yields $`15.0\%`$,
and LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] performs worst at $`16.9\%`$. This
ranking is consistent with the LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] ablation
reported in the main paper, confirming that
Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] is the strongest Verifier regardless of
the target model. Notably, the self-verification setting
(Qwen2.5-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] judging itself) underperforms all
cross-model configurations, reinforcing the finding that an external
Verifier provides more reliable safety judgments than self-evaluation.

#### Emotion Type

Fig. [20](#Pt0.A6.F20 "Figure 20 ‣ Emotion Type ‣ 0.F.2 Ablation Study: Qwen2-VL-7B ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")(b)
reports ASR by emotion quadrant. Negative-Low arousal emotions achieve
the best ASR at $`9.9\%`$, followed by Positive-High ($`10.4\%`$),
Negative-High ($`11.1\%`$), and both Random and Positive-Low tied at
$`11.5\%`$. This corroborates the LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\]
finding that low-arousal negative emotions (e.g., sadness, melancholy)
are the most effective at steering regeneration toward safe outputs,
while random sampling from the full emotion space offers no advantage
over targeted quadrant selection.

![Refer to caption](2607.02089v1/figure/fig_ablation_1_2_qwen.png)

Figure 20: Ablation on Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\]
(VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]). (a) Choice of Verifier:
Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] achieves the lowest ASR ($`11.5\%`$).
(b) Emotion type: Negative-Low emotions yield the best safety ($`9.9\%`$
ASR).

#### Insertion Location

Fig. [21](#Pt0.A6.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ Number of Emotions ‣ 0.F.2 Ablation Study: Qwen2-VL-7B ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")(a)
compares inserting the emotion prompt at the beginning versus the end of
the input, alongside a no-emotion baseline. Beginning insertion achieves
$`9.9\%`$ ASR, outperforming end insertion ($`11.4\%`$) by $`1.5`$ pp.
Both configurations substantially reduce ASR compared to the baseline
without emotion prompting ($`20.0\%`$), representing a $`50.5\%`$
relative reduction for beginning insertion and a $`42.8\%`$ relative
reduction for end insertion. This confirms the finding from
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] that placing the emotion prompt at the
beginning of the input is more effective.

#### Number of Emotions

Fig. [21](#Pt0.A6.F21 "Figure 21 ‣ Number of Emotions ‣ 0.F.2 Ablation Study: Qwen2-VL-7B ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")(b)
varies the number of emotions injected from one to four. A single
emotion achieves the lowest ASR ($`9.9\%`$), while two emotions yield
the highest ($`11.3\%`$). Three and four emotions produce intermediate
results ($`10.7\%`$ and $`10.6\%`$, respectively). This pattern mirrors
the LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] ablation, where increasing the
number of emotions does not improve safety and a single, well-chosen
emotion is optimal.

![Refer to caption](2607.02089v1/figure/fig_ablation_3_4_qwen.png)

Figure 21: Ablation on Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\]
(VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]). (a) Insertion location: beginning
placement reduces ASR by $`10.1`$ pp over the baseline. (b) Number of
emotions: a single emotion is optimal ($`9.9\%`$ ASR).

#### Summary

Across all four ablation dimensions, the optimal ESC configuration on
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] matches the one identified on
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\]: Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] as the
Verifier, a single Negative-Low emotion, inserted at the beginning of
the prompt. This configuration achieves $`9.9\%`$ ASR on
VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\], a $`50.5\%`$ relative reduction from the
$`20.0\%`$ baseline, demonstrating that the ESC design choices are
robust and transfer across model architectures.

### 0.F.3 Pipeline Component Ablation

The ablation studies in Sec. 5.4 of the main paper isolate the
contributions of emotion type, placement, and dosage. Here we complement
those with two *pipeline-level* ablations that test the necessity of the
Verifier $`M_{V}`$’s two roles in the ESC pipeline (Algo. 1 in the main
paper): the *verification stage*, which determines whether revision is
necessary, and the *final comparison*, in which the Verifier compares
the initial and revised responses and chooses the more appropriate one.
All experiments use the optimal configuration identified by the main
ablations: Gemma3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] as the Verifier $`M_{V}`$,
Negative-Low Arousal emotional feedback, insertion at the beginning, and
two emotional cues. We evaluate on VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] using the
same impartial-judge protocol
(Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \[[26](#bib.bib16)\]) as the main experiments.

Setup. We define two ablation variants and compare them against the full
ESC pipeline:

- •
  w/o Verification: The verification stage is removed. All initial
  responses $`R_{initial}`$ are treated as not accepted and sent
  directly to the self-correction stage, where emotional feedback
  $`F_{emotional}`$ is injected and the target VLM $`M_{T}`$ revisits
  its reasoning to produce a revised response $`R_{revised}`$. The final
  comparison is retained.
- •
  w/o Comparison: The final comparison is removed. Whenever the target
  VLM produces a revised response $`R_{revised}`$, it is always returned
  as the final output without the Verifier comparing it against the
  initial response $`R_{initial}`$.

Results.

| Configuration       | LLaVA-1.5-7B | Qwen2-VL-7B |
|---------------------|--------------|-------------|
| Baseline (no ESC)   | 71.6%        | 20.0%       |
| Full ESC            | 25.3%        | 9.9%        |
|    w/o Verification | 29.0%        | 12.3%       |
|    w/o Comparison   | 30.1%        | 10.3%       |

Table 13: Pipeline component ablation on VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]
(ASR $`\downarrow`$, lower is better). Full ESC uses all steps in
Algo. 1 of the main paper. Both ablation variants increase ASR,
confirming that both the verification stage and the final comparison are
necessary.

Analysis. Both ablation variants yield higher ASR than the full
pipeline, confirming that both the verification stage and the final
comparison contribute to ESC’s effectiveness.

*Removing the verification stage* increases ASR by +3.7 pp on
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] (25.3%$`\to`$29.0%) and +2.4 pp on
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] (9.9%$`\to`$12.3%). Without the
verification stage, all initial responses are sent to the
self-correction stage—including those that are already reliable. This
indiscriminate revision introduces two failure modes: (1) reliable
initial responses may be degraded by unnecessary self-correction, and
(2) the Verifier in the final comparison must now evaluate a larger
number of initial–revised pairs, increasing the chance of errors. The
result confirms that the verification stage serves a protective
function: by determining which responses actually need revision, it
prevents the self-correction stage from interfering with
already-reliable outputs.

*Removing the final comparison* increases ASR by +4.8 pp on
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] (25.3%$`\to`$30.1%) and +0.4 pp on
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] (9.9%$`\to`$10.3%). Without the
comparison, every revised response $`R_{revised}`$ is accepted
unconditionally. This reveals that self-correction through emotional
context does not always produce a more appropriate response—in some
cases, the revised output is worse than the initial one. The Verifier’s
comparison acts as a quality gate that catches these regression cases.
The effect is more pronounced on LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\]
(+4.8 pp) than on Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] (+0.4 pp), suggesting
that the model with stronger inherent alignment produces more
consistently improved revisions, reducing the need for post-revision
comparison.

*Cross-model asymmetry.* On LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], removing
the final comparison causes a slightly larger degradation (+4.8 pp) than
removing the verification stage (+3.7 pp). On
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], the pattern reverses: removing
verification (+2.4 pp) hurts more than removing comparison (+0.4 pp).
This asymmetry reflects the models’ different baseline characteristics.
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], with its lower baseline ASR (20.0%),
produces more reliable initial responses; the verification stage
preserves these by avoiding unnecessary revision.
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], with its higher baseline ASR (71.6%),
requires revision more often, making the final comparison more valuable
as a quality gate over the revised outputs.

Overall, these results validate the design of the full ESC pipeline:
both the assess-before-revising mechanism (verification) and the
compare-before-accepting mechanism (final comparison) are necessary for
optimal performance, and neither can be removed without degradation.

### 0.F.4 ESC Pipeline Conversion Funnel

Tab. [14](#Pt0.A6.T14 "Table 14 ‣ Overall intervention. ‣ 0.F.4 ESC Pipeline Conversion Funnel ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
reports the step-by-step conversion statistics of the ESC pipeline
across all benchmarks for both LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], sorted by overall intervention rate.
For each model–benchmark pair we report: (i) the *flag rate*—the
proportion of initial responses $`R_{initial}`$ deemed unacceptable by
$`M_{V}`$ in the verification stage and routed for self-correction;
(ii) the *accept rate*—among flagged samples, the proportion for which
$`M_{V}`$ selected the revised response $`\mathcal{R}_{\text{revised}}`$
over $`R_{initial}`$ in the final comparison stage; and (iii) the
*overall intervention rate*—the net fraction of samples whose final
response is $`\mathcal{R}_{\text{revised}}`$.

##### Flag rate.

Flag rates track benchmark difficulty. For
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], safety-oriented benchmarks attract the
highest flag rates (VLSafe: 84.7%; MMVP: 71.3%), while
perception-grounded tasks produce the lowest (BLINK: 12.9%; POPE:
14.0%). Reasoning benchmarks occupy a middle range (MMMU: 44.5%;
MathVista: 46.9%; AI2D: 40.5%). Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] shows
more uniform flagging (23–75%) with the notable exception of BLINK
(11.9%) and MMStar (23.4%), reflecting its stronger baseline on
structured multi-image and recognition tasks.

##### Accept rate.

The verifier accepts $`\mathcal{R}_{\text{revised}}`$ at consistently
high rates once a sample is flagged. For
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\], accept rates range from 35.2% (POPE)
to 90.4% (VLSafe), with reasoning benchmarks in the 66–69% range (MMMU:
66.1%; AI2D: 68.5%; MathVista: 67.8%). For
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\], accept rates are higher overall
(32–94%), peaking at 93.9% on MM-Vet and 89.5% on MMVP. BLINK is the
sole outlier for Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] (32.2%), where the
multi-image reasoning format limits the verifier’s ability to
discriminate between responses.

##### Overall intervention.

ESC intervenes most heavily where baseline performance is weakest.
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] sees the largest intervention on VLSafe
(76.6%) and MMVP (52.3%); Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] on MM-Vet
(70.6%) and MMSafetyBench (40.7%). Reasoning benchmarks show moderate
intervention rates (MMMU: 29.4%/23.7%; AI2D: 27.8%/23.0% for
LLaVA/Qwen2), consistent with the moderate gains reported in
Tab. [2](#S5.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 5.2 Quantitative Results ‣ 5 Experimental Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
At the other extreme, BLINK and POPE show the lowest intervention rates
for LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] (7.1% and 4.9%), and BLINK for
Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\] (3.8%), confirming that ESC selectively
corrects responses rather than indiscriminately replacing them.

|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  |  | \# | Rate | \# | Accept | Intervention |
| LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] | VLSafe | 1110 | 940 | 84.7% | 850 | 90.4% | 76.6% |
|  | MMVP | 300 | 214 | 71.3% | 157 | 73.4% | 52.3% |
|  | MMSafetyBench | 5040 | 2666 | 52.9% | 1637 | 61.4% | 32.5% |

unacceptable by $`M_{V}`$ in the verification stage. *Revised*
($`\mathcal{R}_{\text{revised}}`$): flagged samples for which $`M_{V}`$

stage. MMSafetyBench counts are summed across three subsets (SD,

### 0.F.5 Iterative Self-Correction

The ESC pipeline described in Algo. 1 applies a single
detect-then-regenerate loop. A natural extension is to ask whether
applying ESC *iteratively*—feeding each loop’s corrected responses as
input to the next—yields cumulative safety improvements. We investigate
this by running four successive loops of the full ESC pipeline on
VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\] ($`n{=}1{,}110`$) for both
LLaVA-1.5-7B \[[50](#bib.bib7)\] and Qwen2-VL-7B \[[92](#bib.bib6)\],
using Gemma-3-12B \[[82](#bib.bib17)\] as the verifier $`M_{V}`$, with
fixed negative-low arousal emotion prompts ($`k{=}2`$) inserted at the
beginning.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | Counts |  |  |  | Rates (%) |  |
| $`M_{T}`$ | Loop | Safe | Unsafe | Uncertain | Total | ASR$`\downarrow`$ | $`\Delta`$ASR (pp) |
| LLaVA-1.5 | 1 | 765 | 327 | 18 | 1110 | 29.5 | — |
|  | 2 | 798 | 304 | 8 | 1110 | 27.4 | $`-2.1`$ |
|  | 3 | 811 | 293 | 6 | 1110 | 26.4 | $`-1.0`$ |
|  | 4 | 819 | 286 | 5 | 1110 | 25.8 | $`-0.6`$ |
| Qwen2-VL | 1 | 981 | 125 | 4 | 1110 | 11.3 | — |
|  | 2 | 988 | 120 | 2 | 1110 | 10.8 | $`-0.5`$ |
|  | 3 | 990 | 117 | 3 | 1110 | 10.5 | $`-0.3`$ |
|  | 4 | 993 | 114 | 3 | 1110 | 10.3 | $`-0.2`$ |

Table 15: Iterative self-correction on VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]
($`M_{V}`$: Gemma-3-12B, $`n{=}1{,}110`$, negative-low quadrant,
$`k{=}2`$ emotions). ASR: Attack Success Rate (%).

Results are summarized in
Tab. [15](#Pt0.A6.T15 "Table 15 ‣ 0.F.5 Iterative Self-Correction ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models")
and
Fig. [22](#Pt0.A6.F22 "Figure 22 ‣ 0.F.5 Iterative Self-Correction ‣ Appendix 0.F More Quantitative Results ‣ ESC : Emotional Self-Correction for Reliable Vision Language Models").
For LLaVA-1.5-7B, ASR decreases monotonically from 29.5% after the first
loop to 25.8% after the fourth, a cumulative reduction of 3.7 percentage
points. The largest single-loop gain occurs between loops 1 and 2
($`\Delta`$ASR $`={-}2.1`$ pp), with diminishing returns in subsequent
iterations ($`-1.0`$ pp and $`-0.6`$ pp for loops 2$`\to`$3 and
3$`\to`$4, respectively).

For Qwen2-VL-7B, whose baseline ASR after loop 1 is already low at
11.3%, iterative correction still yields consistent reductions, reaching
10.3% by loop 4 ($`-1.0`$ pp total). The per-loop gains are smaller
($`-0.5`$, $`-0.3`$, $`-0.2`$ pp), reflecting the model’s stronger
inherent safety alignment—consistent with the pattern observed in
Sec. 5.2, where Qwen2-VL-7B exhibits lower baseline vulnerability across
benchmarks.

The contrasting magnitudes across the two models are informative.
LLaVA-1.5-7B, which starts with a high ASR, benefits substantially from
iterative correction: the residual unsafe pool shrinks by 41 samples
(327$`\to`$286) across four loops. Qwen2-VL-7B, starting from a much
lower ASR, shows smaller absolute gains (125$`\to`$114), suggesting that
the remaining adversarial samples resist emotion-based correction under
the current configuration. Both models exhibit diminishing returns,
indicating convergence toward a residual set of hard samples. These
results demonstrate that ESC can be applied as a multi-pass
inference-time strategy with consistent, though diminishing, cumulative
gains—without any modification to model weights.

![Refer to caption](2607.02089v1/figure/loop_convergence.png)

Figure 22: Iterative self-correction convergence on
VLSafe \[[10](#bib.bib23)\]. (a) LLaVA-1.5-7B and (b) Qwen2-VL-7B. Red:
ASR ($`\downarrow`$ better); green: safe rate ($`\uparrow`$ better).
